> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Application: What is the productive failure effect, and what is its proposed mechanism?
> **A:** **Back:** Productive failure (Kapur) is the finding that having learners attempt to solve complex problems before instruction — generating imperfect or failed solutions — significantly enhances subsequent learning from explicit instruction compared to instruction-first sequences. Proposed mechanisms: (1) activates relevant prior knowledge schemas; (2) creates productive awareness of existing schemas' limitations (disequilibrium); (3) differentiates the problem features needing explanation; (4) generates anticipatory attention that makes subsequent instruction more meaningful and better integrated.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition: What is legitimate peripheral participation?
> **A:** **Back:** Legitimate peripheral participation (Lave & Wenger, 1991) is the entry mode for novice learners in a community of practice: the newcomer participates in community activities in a limited, peripheral capacity — with genuine but partial access to the community's tools, language, norms, and knowledge — and through progressively more central participation develops full community competence. It is "legitimate" because it is a genuine, recognized form of community membership; "peripheral" because it begins at the margins; and "participation" because learning is fundamentally about doing, not knowing.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Definition — Core Principle
> **A:** **Q:** What does the Encoding Specificity Principle assert is the primary determinant of successful memory retrieval?
> **A:** The match between the information present at retrieval and the information encoded at the time of learning. Specifically, a retrieval cue will facilitate recall if and only if information about the cue and its relationship to the target was encoded at study. Trace strength alone is insufficient.
> **Source:** Section 1.2
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #esp #memory #retrieval-principle

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Distinction — ESP vs. Trace Strength
> **A:** **Q:** What finding by Tulving and Thomson (1973) most directly contradicted strength-based models of memory?
> **A:** "Recognition failure of recallable words" — items that participants failed to recognize on a standard recognition test (presented with a strong pre-experimental associate) were nevertheless successfully recalled when cued with the weaker but *encoded* context word. Strength models predict recognition ≥ recall performance; the ESP correctly predicts the opposite when test conditions fail to match encoding conditions.
> **Source:** Section 4.1
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #esp #recognition-failure #encoding-retrieval-match

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Mechanism — Synergistic Ecphory
> **A:** **Q:** What does "synergistic" mean in Tulving's synergistic ecphory, and why does it matter?
> **A:** "Synergistic" means the retrieval output is the joint product of the stored engram (trace) and the retrieval cue — neither alone determines what is remembered. It matters because it means retrieval is constructive rather than reproductive: the same stored trace can yield different retrieval outputs depending on which cue initiates ecphory. Memory is not a readout; it is a reconstruction.
> **Source:** Section 3.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #ecphory #esp #constructive-memory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Process — Hippocampal Reinstatement
> **A:** **Q:** What is hippocampal reinstatement, and how does it support the Encoding Specificity Principle?
> **A:** Hippocampal reinstatement is the phenomenon, documented via fMRI, whereby the neural activity pattern during successful memory retrieval recapitulates the pattern present during encoding. It supports the ESP by providing direct neural evidence that retrieval success involves recreating the encoding context — not merely activating a trace — confirming the cue-engram resonance model at the neural level.
> **Source:** Section 4.4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #hippocampus #reinstatement #esp #neuroscience

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Distinction — ESP vs. Transfer-Appropriate Processing
> **A:** **Q:** How do the Encoding Specificity Principle and Transfer-Appropriate Processing differ in their account of encoding-retrieval compatibility?
> **A:** The ESP defines compatibility at the *content level*: specific encoded cue-target relationships must be present at retrieval. TAP defines compatibility at the *process level*: the type of cognitive operation (phonological, semantic) at test must match the type at study. They make overlapping predictions in most real-world conditions but diverge when processing type matches but encoded content does not. TAP is procedural; the ESP is content-specific.
> **Source:** Section 5.2
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #esp #transfer-appropriate-processing #encoding-retrieval-match

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Application — Context Reinstatement in Cognitive Interview
> **A:** **Q:** How does the Cognitive Interview technique apply the Encoding Specificity Principle?
> **A:** The Cognitive Interview instructs eyewitnesses to mentally reinstate the context of the witnessed event — not just its content, but the sensory environment, their physical state, and emotional state at the time. This mental context reinstatement provides retrieval cues that match the encoded context of the event, facilitating more complete ecphoric output. Geiselman & Fisher's research showed this significantly improves witness recall quality.
> **Source:** Section 7.5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #esp #cognitive-interview #forensic-memory #applied

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Connection — ESP and Desirable Difficulties
> **A:** **Q:** How does the ESP explain why spacing (distributed practice) typically improves long-term retention?
> **A:** Under the ESP framework, spacing may improve retention by encoding the target material across multiple temporal and contextual states. Retrieval after forgetting exposes the learner to a retrieval challenge; successful retrieval in that state adds the "retrieval-at-varied-temporal-context" as an encoded attribute. The distributed trace is therefore richer in contextual variation, making it accessible from a wider range of future retrieval contexts than a massed trace that was only ever encoded in one contextual state.
> **Source:** Sections 6, 8.4; [[spaced-repetition]], [[Robert-Bjork]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #esp #spaced-repetition #desirable-difficulties #encoding

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Boundary — When Context Effects Are Smaller
> **A:** **Q:** Under what conditions are encoding specificity / context-dependent memory effects reduced or eliminated?
> **A:** Context-dependent effects are attenuated when: (a) participants use deliberate mental context reinstatement (Smith, 1979, 1986); (b) material is tested via recognition rather than recall (recognition provides its own cue); (c) material is deeply semantically elaborated (creating more context-flexible traces); (d) material is highly automatized or proceduralized (procedural memory is less context-bound than episodic). These boundary conditions refine rather than refute the ESP — they specify when context is a primary determinant vs. when other factors dominate.
> **Source:** Sections 4.5, 8.1
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #esp #boundary-conditions #context-dependent-memory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Definition — Ecphory
> **A:** **Q:** Define ecphory in Tulving's sense, distinguishing it from simple trace activation.
> **A:** Ecphory (from Semon, revived by Tulving) is the process by which a retrieval cue interacts with a stored engram to bring a memory to consciousness. It is not simple trace activation because the cue participates in shaping the retrieval output, not merely triggering a pre-formed record. Ecphory is synergistic: the output depends on the joint properties of cue and trace, not either alone.
> **Source:** Section 3.1
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #ecphory #esp #memory-mechanism

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition — Generative Learning Theory** | Q: What is the core thesis of Generative Learning Theory, and who developed it?
> **A:** Developed by Merlin Wittrock (1974), GLT proposes that learning is an active process through which the learner constructs meaning by generating connections between new information and prior knowledge through the processes of selection, organization, and integration (SOI). The learner does not record information but builds mental models through active cognitive construction.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition — Generation Effect** | Q: What is the generation effect, and who first systematically documented it?
> **A:** The generation effect (Slamecka & Graf, 1978) is the empirical finding that generating information from minimal cues produces better memory retention than passively reading the same information. It is one of educational psychology's most replicated findings and provides direct empirical support for GLT's core claim about active versus passive encoding.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition — Generativity Paradox** | Q: What is the generativity paradox, and how is it resolved?
> **A:** The apparent contradiction between GLT's prescription for active generation and CLT's warning that cognitive demand impairs learning. Resolved by distinguishing load types: generative activities increase *germane* load (schema-building effort), not extraneous load. The constraint is that total load must stay within working memory capacity — a constraint dependent on learner prior knowledge and the material's intrinsic complexity.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Distinction — Note-Taking vs. Note-Making** | Q: How do GLT and the note-taking research distinguish note-taking from note-making, and which produces deeper learning?
> **A:** Note-taking = passive transcription, minimal cognitive reorganization. Note-making = active transformation — reorganizing, paraphrasing, connecting to prior knowledge, and generating one's own structures. GLT predicts and Mueller & Oppenheimer (2014) demonstrate that note-making (exemplified by longhand's forced condensation) produces superior conceptual learning because it requires the organizing and integrating processes that build mental models.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Concept — Expertise Reversal Effect** | Q: What is the expertise reversal effect, and what does it imply for generative strategy prescription?
> **A:** The expertise reversal effect (Kalyuga et al., 2003): instructional methods optimal for novices may impair experts, and vice versa. For generative strategies, this implies that strategies highly effective for intermediate/advanced learners may impose unsupportable cognitive load on novices who lack the foundational schemas to make generation productive. No universal optimal strategy exists — only strategies optimal for a particular learner at a particular expertise stage.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Concept — Productive Failure** | Q: What is productive failure and what learning mechanism does it exploit?
> **A:** Kapur's instructional design principle: structure learning so students attempt complex problems *before* formal instruction. The pre-instruction struggle (usually including incorrect attempts) is "productive" because it activates, differentiates, and organizes prior knowledge relevant to the target concept, creating a "prepared mind" for instruction by establishing what needs to be explained. Exploits GLT's selection-organization-integration mechanism at the level of prior knowledge preparation.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Concept — Desirable Difficulties** | Q: What are desirable difficulties and why does subjective difficulty predict learning benefit?
> **A:** Bjork's concept: conditions that increase short-term effort and difficulty (spaced practice, interleaved practice, reduced feedback) improve long-term retention and transfer. Difficulty is "desirable" when it increases *germane* cognitive load — effort invested in schema construction — rather than extraneous load. Subjective difficulty often signals productive generative processing precisely because generation is cognitively demanding, and cognitive demand is the signature of schema-building work.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Process — CTML's Three Assumptions** | Q: What are the three foundational assumptions of Mayer's Cognitive Theory of Multimedia Learning?
> **A:** (1) Dual Channel — humans have separate processing channels for verbal/auditory and visual/pictorial information; (2) Limited Capacity — each channel can process only a limited number of elements simultaneously; (3) Active Processing — meaningful learning requires active selection, organization into verbal and pictorial mental models, and integration of those models with each other and with prior knowledge. These map directly onto Wittrock's SOI model within Baddeley's architectural framework.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Application — Self-Explanation Strategy** | Q: How should self-explanation be deployed as a study strategy, and what conditions maximize its benefit?
> **A:** Self-explain by pausing while studying worked examples or complex texts to verbalize (or write) *why* each step is valid, what principle it illustrates, and how it connects to prior knowledge. Maximize benefit by: (1) using for material with inferential gaps rather than fully explicit text; (2) building sufficient prior knowledge first (self-explanation requires something to integrate with); (3) comparing your explanation against the source to identify errors; (4) focusing on causal and structural relationships, not surface description.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Connection — GLT and Retrieval Practice** | Q: How does retrieval practice implement GLT's generative learning principle?
> **A:** Retrieval practice (self-testing, spaced repetition, practice tests) is generatively superior to re-reading because retrieval requires the active reconstruction of information from memory — engaging the selection and integration processes that constitute generative encoding. Each retrieval attempt strengthens both the memory trace and the retrieval routes to it. GLT explains *why* the well-replicated testing effect works: retrieval is inherently a generative act, not a passive one.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Distinction — Germane vs. Extraneous Cognitive Load** | Q: How do germane and extraneous cognitive load relate to GLT's prescription for generative activity?
> **A:** Extraneous load = effort wasted on processing irrelevant elements of the learning environment (bad design, split attention, redundancy). Germane load = effort directly invested in schema construction and automation. GLT promotes increasing germane load through generative strategies. CLT advises eliminating extraneous load to preserve working memory capacity for germane processing. The two frameworks complement when properly distinguished: both aim to maximize the proportion of available cognitive resources devoted to actual schema building.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Concept — ICAP Hierarchy and Constructive Activities** | Q: What distinguishes Constructive from Active activities in Chi's ICAP framework, and why does this distinction matter for GLT?
> **A:** Active activities (highlighting, copying, underlining) engage the learner behaviorally but do not produce outputs that go beyond the presented information — they manipulate surface structure without genuinely transforming it. Constructive activities (summarizing, self-explaining, concept mapping, elaborating) require producing outputs that *add to* the presented information — generating inferences, connections, organizations, and elaborations not present in the source. This distinction matters for GLT because it identifies that the critical threshold for generative processing is the production of learner-generated content that exceeds the information encountered, not mere behavioral engagement with the material.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Card 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fundamental distinction between declarative and non-declarative long-term memory?
> **A:** Declarative memory supports conscious, intentional recollection of facts (semantic) and events (episodic); non-declarative memory encompasses learning expressed through performance changes (skills, habits, priming, conditioning) without requiring conscious awareness.
> **Source:** Section II
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #ltm-taxonomy, #declarative-memory, #non-declarative-memory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Card 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** How does the levels-of-processing framework explain differences in memory durability?
> **A:** Memory durability depends on the depth of cognitive processing at encoding. Shallow processing (attending to perceptual features) produces weak traces; deep processing (attending to meaning, generating elaborations) produces durable traces. The key variable is processing quality, not study time.
> **Source:** Section III
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #encoding, #levels-of-processing, #craik-lockhart

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Card 3 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What are the two levels of memory consolidation, and how do they differ in timescale and mechanism?
> **A:** Synaptic consolidation (minutes to hours) involves molecular changes at synapses including protein synthesis and receptor modification. Systems consolidation (weeks to years) involves the gradual reorganisation of memory representations from hippocampal to neocortical networks, with sleep-dependent replay as a key mechanism.
> **Source:** Section IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #consolidation, #synaptic-consolidation, #systems-consolidation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Card 4 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between the standard model of consolidation and multiple trace theory?
> **A:** The standard model holds that consolidated memories become fully hippocampally independent over time. Multiple trace theory argues that the hippocampus remains permanently necessary for detailed episodic retrieval; only semantic/gist information becomes hippocampally independent. The key disagreement concerns whether remote episodic memories can survive hippocampal damage.
> **Source:** Section IV
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #consolidation, #standard-model, #multiple-trace-theory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Card 5 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What is the testing effect, and why does it work?
> **A:** The testing effect is the finding that retrieving information from memory enhances long-term retention more than re-studying. It works because retrieval strengthens and elaborates the retrieved trace, increases the number of retrieval routes, and provides diagnostic information about what is and is not well-learned.
> **Source:** Section VIII
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #testing-effect, #retrieval-practice, #desirable-difficulties

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Card 6 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the encoding specificity principle constrain retrieval?
> **A:** The encoding specificity principle holds that a retrieval cue is effective only to the extent that it matches the information encoded alongside the target at learning. Retrieval success depends on cue-trace compatibility, not just trace strength — explaining why environmental context, internal state, and processing mode at retrieval all affect memory performance.
> **Source:** Section VI
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #encoding-specificity, #retrieval, #cue-dependent-memory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Card 7 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is reconstructive memory, and how does it differ from a reproductive model?
> **A:** Reconstructive memory is the principle that retrieval assembles a plausible account from stored fragments, schemas, and contextual influences — rather than playing back a stored copy (reproductive model). Reconstruction enables inference and generalization but introduces vulnerability to distortion, gap-filling, and misinformation incorporation.
> **Source:** Section VII
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #reconstructive-memory, #bartlett, #schema-theory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Card 8 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does schema theory connect encoding, storage, and retrieval processes?
> **A:** Schemas shape all three: at encoding, schemas determine what is attended to and how it is interpreted; in storage, schema-consistent information is preferentially retained and schema-inconsistent information may be distorted; at retrieval, schemas guide reconstruction and fill gaps. This makes schemas both the primary organising mechanism of LTM and the primary source of systematic memory distortion.
> **Source:** Sections III, V, VII
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #schema-theory, #encoding, #storage, #retrieval

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Card 9 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes the spacing effect from the testing effect, and how do they interact?
> **A:** The spacing effect concerns the distribution of study episodes over time (spaced > massed). The testing effect concerns the activity during study (retrieval > re-study). They interact synergistically: spaced retrieval practice produces greater long-term retention than either spacing or testing alone, forming the foundation of spaced repetition systems.
> **Source:** Section VIII
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #spacing-effect, #testing-effect, #spaced-repetition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Card 10 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** Why do "desirable difficulties" enhance learning despite reducing immediate performance?
> **A:** Desirable difficulties (spacing, interleaving, generation, retrieval practice) force deeper processing and more effortful retrieval, which strengthens the memory trace and creates more flexible, transferable representations. Immediate performance suffers because the effortful processing is harder than fluent re-reading, but long-term retention and transfer are enhanced. The key insight is that ease of learning ≠ quality of learning.
> **Source:** Section VIII
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #desirable-difficulties, #bjork, #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 1 — Definition: Metacognition
> **A:** **Q:** What is Flavell's definition of metacognition, and what two broad categories does it comprise?
> **A:** Metacognition is cognition about cognition — knowledge and processes that take one's own cognitive states and products as their objects. It comprises (a) metacognitive knowledge (stored declarative knowledge about person, task, and strategy factors affecting cognition) and (b) metacognitive experiences (real-time monitoring signals arising during cognitive engagement).
> **Source:** Section 2; Flavell (1979)
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** definition, metacognition, Flavell

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 2 — Framework: Nelson-Narens
> **A:** **Q:** Describe the two levels and two information flows in the Nelson-Narens metacognitive monitoring-control framework.
> **A:** Object level: first-order cognitive processes (studying, reasoning, retrieving). Meta level: metacognitive processes that observe and control the object level. Upward flow (monitoring): the meta level receives information about the object level's state (via JOLs, FOK, EOL). Downward flow (control): the meta level sends commands that modify object-level processes (study more, stop, seek help).
> **Source:** Section 3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** framework, Nelson-Narens, monitoring, control

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 3 — Distinction: Monitoring vs. Regulation
> **A:** **Q:** How do metacognitive monitoring and regulation differ, and why does the monitoring-control gap matter?
> **A:** Monitoring is the metacognitive assessment of one's current cognitive state (JOL, FOK, comprehension check — generating information). Regulation is the behavioral response to that information (study more, switch strategies, seek help — executing change). The monitoring-control gap describes findings that accurate monitoring does not automatically translate into appropriate regulation — learners may detect a comprehension gap without changing their study approach, because regulation is a separate capacity that can fail independently of monitoring.
> **Source:** Sections 3, 5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** monitoring, regulation, distinction, monitoring-control gap

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 4 — Mechanism: Fluency Illusion
> **A:** **Q:** Why does rereading feel more productive than retrieval practice, and why is this feeling misleading?
> **A:** Rereading familiar material is processed fluently (quickly, easily, without retrieval failure), and the monitoring system interprets this processing fluency as evidence of deep comprehension — the fluency illusion. Retrieval practice involves frequent retrieval failure, which the monitoring system correctly interprets as difficulty but incorrectly interprets as ineffective learning. In reality, the storage-retrieval paradox means the effortful encoding of retrieval practice produces more durable memory traces, while the fluent rereading of familiar material produces superficial reactivation without strengthened storage.
> **Source:** Sections 4, 7, 8
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** fluency illusion, desirable difficulties, retrieval practice, mechanism

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 5 — Process: Zimmerman's SRL Cycle
> **A:** **Q:** Name the three phases of Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model and identify one metacognitive process in each.
> **A:** (1) Forethought: task analysis and strategic planning before task engagement — selecting an evidence-based study strategy based on task demands and goals. (2) Performance: self-monitoring and self-instruction during task engagement — detecting comprehension failures and adjusting reading strategy in response. (3) Self-reflection: self-evaluation and causal attribution after task completion — assessing whether performance met goals and attributing gaps to modifiable (strategy, preparation) rather than fixed (ability) causes.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** Zimmerman, SRL, three-phase cycle, regulation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 6 — Concept: Desirable Difficulties
> **A:** **Q:** Name three desirable difficulties and explain one structural reason they improve long-term retention despite feeling less effective.
> **A:** Three desirable difficulties: (1) retrieval practice (testing rather than restudying), (2) interleaving (mixing problem types during practice), (3) spaced practice (distributing study across time). Structural reason: the storage-retrieval paradox — conditions that require effortful encoding (which creates the sensation of difficulty) produce more elaborated and robustly linked memory traces that are accessible under a wider range of retrieval cues. The difficulty signals effort during encoding, not poor learning.
> **Source:** Section 7; Bjork & Bjork (2011)
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** desirable difficulties, Bjork, retrieval practice, spacing

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 7 — Developmental: Metacognitive Inertia
> **A:** **Q:** What is metacognitive inertia, and what is its educational implication?
> **A:** Metacognitive inertia is the developmental state in which strategies and metacognitive knowledge exist in the learner's repertoire but are not spontaneously deployed without explicit prompting (Ann Brown). Young children are paradigmatically inert: they follow a strategy when prompted but do not initiate it. Educational implication: strategy instruction must include not only the what and how but the conditional knowledge of when and why — to build the spontaneous triggering that converts inert strategy knowledge into deployed strategy use.
> **Source:** Section 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** Ann Brown, metacognitive inertia, development, strategy instruction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 8 — Failure Mode: Dunning-Kruger Mechanism
> **A:** **Q:** What metacognitive mechanism underlies the Dunning-Kruger effect, and why does it affect beginners in any domain?
> **A:** The mechanism: the metacognitive skills required to recognize a correct answer and to accurately assess one's own performance quality are the same domain-specific knowledge and skills. Low performers lack not only the domain knowledge to perform accurately but the domain knowledge to monitor their performance accurately — they cannot see what they cannot see. This is not a personality trait of incompetent people but a structural feature of early-stage learning: everyone shows the Dunning-Kruger pattern in domains where they lack expertise, because expert monitoring requires expert knowledge.
> **Source:** Section 8; Kruger & Dunning (1999)
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** Dunning-Kruger, failure modes, mechanism, monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 9 — Distinction: Conditional vs. Declarative Metacognitive Knowledge
> **A:** **Q:** Distinguish declarative from conditional metacognitive knowledge and explain which is more predictive of strategy transfer.
> **A:** Declarative metacognitive knowledge is knowing that a strategy exists ("retrieval practice is a learning strategy"). Conditional metacognitive knowledge is knowing when and why to apply it ("retrieval practice is most beneficial when the goal is durable long-term retrieval rather than immediate recognition, and when material has been study once to initial encoding threshold"). Research (Paris, Lipson, and Wixson) shows conditional knowledge is more predictive of spontaneous strategy transfer than declarative knowledge alone — because conditional knowledge provides the context triggers and rationale that prompt spontaneous deployment in novel situations.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** conditional knowledge, declarative knowledge, transfer, strategy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-skills-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 10 — Application: Growth Mindset as Metacognitive Intervention
> **A:** **Q:** How can growth mindset interventions be understood as metacognitive knowledge interventions?
> **A:** Growth mindset (incremental intelligence theory) interventions shift the person knowledge layer of metacognitive knowledge — specifically, the learner's model of the nature of intelligence and the modifiability of their own cognitive capacities. Entity theory (fixed intelligence) makes difficulty signals trigger threat responses (monitoring "I'm confused" → interpretation "I'm not smart enough"); incremental theory makes the same difficulty signals trigger mastery responses ("I'm confused → this is an opportunity to learn"). The metacognitive mechanism is reinterpretation of monitoring signals through a different knowledge framework, not a change in monitoring capacity itself.
> **Source:** Section 7; Blackwell et al. (2007)
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** growth mindset, Dweck, metacognitive knowledge, person knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the distinction between textbase and situation model in Kintsch's reading comprehension model?
> **A:** The textbase is a coherent propositional representation of *what the text says* — the literal semantic content of the text's sentences. The situation model is a mental representation of *what the text describes* — the state of affairs in the world or domain the text refers to. Genuine comprehension requires situation model construction; textbase representation alone supports recognition but not transfer or integration.
> **Source:** Section 2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #comprehension #reading-theory #situation-model

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion and why is it motivationally significant?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is the metacognitive error of mistaking the ease of text processing (reading fluency) for depth of comprehension (situation model construction). It is motivationally significant because it prevents deployment of deeper processing strategies (if the reader believes they understand, they stop working to understand), produces false assessment confidence, and when assessment performance fails, this failure is misattributed to ability rather than monitoring — reinforcing entity theories and performance-avoidance goals.
> **Source:** Sections 2 & 7
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognition #fluency-illusion #comprehension-monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three basic psychological needs in SDT, and how does each apply specifically to academic reading?
> **A:** (1) **Autonomy** — the need to experience reading as self-initiated and volitionally chosen. In reading: supporting autonomy means providing genuine rationales, meaningful choice within required content, and framing reading for personal understanding rather than external compliance. (2) **Competence** — the need to experience efficacy and mastery. In reading: satisfied by successful comprehension experiences, progressive difficulty calibration, and strategy training that produces genuine understanding. (3) **Relatedness** — the need for connection with others. In reading: satisfied by shared reading practices, discussion communities, and meaningful connection of reading content to valued relationships and communities.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #SDT #basic-psychological-needs #reading-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Distinguish mastery-approach, mastery-avoidance, performance-approach, and performance-avoidance goals in the context of academic reading.
> **A:** **Mastery-approach:** Reading to develop genuine understanding; predicts deep processing, persistence, positive affect. **Mastery-avoidance:** Reading to avoid failure-to-understand, driven by comprehension anxiety; predicts self-monitoring and persistence from fear. **Performance-approach:** Reading to demonstrate superior comprehension relative to others; predicts adequate assessed performance but surface strategic reading. **Performance-avoidance:** Reading to avoid appearing incompetent; predicts avoidance, surface processing, learned helplessness — most consistently negative outcome profile.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #achievement-goal-theory #goal-orientation #reading-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the logic of the three-tier strategy hierarchy and what does it mean to "start at the wrong tier"?
> **A:** The hierarchy sequences strategies according to prerequisite logic: Tier 1 (cognitive foundation) creates conditions Tier 2 presupposes; Tier 2 (comprehension strategies) creates conditions Tier 3 presupposes. Starting at the wrong tier means applying strategies that require conditions not yet in place. Example: teaching elaborative interrogation (Tier 2) to a reader with profound vocabulary gaps (Tier 1 deficit) will fail because the reader cannot generate meaningful elaborations when basic comprehension is blocked by unknown vocabulary. The diagnostic must precede the prescription.
> **Source:** Section 6
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #strategy-hierarchy #reading-motivation #intervention-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is self-explanation as a reading strategy and why is it more effective than summarization?
> **A:** Self-explanation involves generating explanatory verbalizations or written articulations of what a passage means in the reader's own words, how it relates to prior content and knowledge, and what gaps remain. It is more effective than summarization because it requires construction of causal relationships and inferential connections that the text may not state explicitly — forcing situation model construction rather than accurate textbase restatement. Summarization can be achieved at the textbase level; genuine self-explanation requires situation model access.
> **Source:** Section 6.3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #self-explanation #comprehension-strategy #situation-model

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model and what are the three phases applied to academic reading?
> **A:** **Forethought phase:** Before reading — goal setting (what do I want to genuinely understand?), strategic planning (which strategies are appropriate for this text?), motivational self-management (intrinsic goal activation, prior knowledge preparation). **Performance phase:** During reading — strategy deployment, comprehension monitoring, fix-up strategy selection and execution when failures are detected. **Self-reflection phase:** After reading — self-evaluation of comprehension quality, causal attribution of successes and failures, adaptive planning for the next reading session. The cycle makes self-regulated reading self-improving rather than repetitively consistent.
> **Source:** Section 7.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #self-regulated-learning #metacognition #reading-self-regulation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is interest theory's four-phase model and what distinguishes situational from individual interest?
> **A:** Four phases: (1) Triggered situational interest — externally triggered by environmental features; (2) Maintained situational interest — sustained by engaging content value; (3) Emerging individual interest — beginning of stored value and re-engagement tendency; (4) Well-developed individual interest — stable, self-sustaining predisposition to engage. **Situational interest** is triggered by specific contextual features (an engaging text, a surprising demonstration) and dissipates when those features are absent. **Individual interest** is a relatively stable personal predisposition that motivates seeking out domain-relevant content across contexts, independent of specific environmental triggers.
> **Source:** Section 3.3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #interest-theory #situational-interest #individual-interest #reading-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Why is retrieval practice more effective than re-reading for long-term retention, and how should it be applied during academic reading?
> **A:** Retrieval practice is more effective because the act of retrieving strengthens the memory trace through effortful reconstruction (a desirable difficulty that enhances encoding), whereas re-reading produces recognition familiarity (high processing fluency) without effortful retrieval — it improves performance on immediate tests but not long-term retention. **Application during reading:** After completing a section, close or cover the text and write from memory the main points and key arguments. Check accuracy against the text. The gaps identified during retrieval attempts are precisely the content requiring focused re-reading or further elaboration.
> **Source:** Sections 6.3 and 7.3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #retrieval-practice #spaced-repetition #desirable-difficulties #reading-strategy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Motivational-Cognitive Reading Stack and what diagnostic implication does it carry?
> **A:** The Stack integrates four systems that must each meet threshold development for motivated academic reading to be self-sustaining: **Layer 1** (Cognitive Pre-Conditions: fluency, vocabulary, working memory management, prior knowledge), **Layer 2** (Comprehension-Generating Strategies: self-explanation, elaborative interrogation, retrieval practice), **Layer 3** (Motivation Architecture: autonomous regulation, mastery goals, intrinsic goal framing, individual interest). **Diagnostic implication:** Identify the lowest layer where the system is failing. A reader who cannot sustain motivation despite good comprehension skills needs Layer 3 work. A reader who is motivated but comprehends shallowly needs Layer 2 strategy training. A reader whose strategies fail needs Layer 1 development. Starting intervention at the correct layer is essential.
> **Source:** Section 6.4 original synthesis
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #motivational-cognitive-stack #original-synthesis #reading-motivation #diagnostic

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Definition):** What is the Motivational Deployment Problem, and how does it differ from strategy ignorance or strategic incapacity?
> 
> **Answer:** The Motivational Deployment Problem is the structural gap between knowing effective learning strategies work and consistently deploying them. It differs from strategy ignorance (not knowing what works) and strategic incapacity (lacking the skill) in that the learner has both knowledge and capacity but fails to deploy due to motivational barriers — primarily the aversive phenomenological experience of the strategies that work best.
> 
> **Source:** Section 1: The Motivation-Strategy Nexus
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #motivation #learning-science #strategy-deployment

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Distinction):** How does autonomous motivation differ from controlled motivation in its effect on the SRL monitoring phase?
> 
> **Answer:** Autonomous motivation supports honest monitoring: comprehension failure is experienced as information, not threat, enabling accurate self-assessment. Controlled motivation produces ego-defensive monitoring: learners subjectively upgrade comprehension estimates under introjected regulation (where self-esteem depends on performance), filtering out monitoring signals that would damage self-concept. Same monitoring capacity; opposite willingness to attend to what it reveals.
> 
> **Source:** Section 5.2: Where SDT Intersects the SRL Cycle
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #self-determination-theory #self-regulated-learning #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Process):** Walk through the hierarchical order of the seven learning effects, explaining why the ordering reflects both cognitive depth and motivational cost.
> 
> **Answer:** Generation (pause, produce) → Testing Effect (retrieve, tolerate failure) → Spacing (tolerate forgetting intervals) → Interleaving (tolerate productive confusion) → Elaboration (sustain effortful connection-making) → Self-Explanation (confront comprehension gaps) → Metacognitive Strategy (regulate all others honestly). Each requires progressively more: more working memory demand, more tolerance of aversive experience, more SRL phase engagement, and more motivational infrastructure. The hierarchical ordering means lower strategies are prerequisite motivational entry points for higher strategies.
> 
> **Source:** Section 3: The Seven Learning Strategies
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #learning-effects #hierarchy #desirable-difficulties

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Definition):** What is the desirable difficulties paradox, and why does it create a persistent motivational problem?
> 
> **Answer:** The desirable difficulties paradox is the finding that the conditions which feel most like productive learning (massed practice, re-reading, blocked practice) are among the least effective for long-term retention, while conditions that feel like ineffective or failed learning (spaced practice, retrieval failure, interleaving confusion) are the most effective. The persistent motivational problem is this: learners use subjective sense of learning as their primary feedback signal for strategy effectiveness, but this signal is systematically inverted for the strategies that matter most.
> 
> **Source:** Section 2.4: Cognitive Load and the Learning Process
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #desirable-difficulties #metacognitive-distortion #strategy-selection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Application):** You have just completed reading a complex 5000-word report on cognitive science. Design the optimal post-reading protocol using specific learning effects to maximize long-term retention.
> 
> **Answer:** (1) **Free recall test** (testing effect at maximum): Close the report and write everything you can recall, without aids, for 15 minutes. (2) **Gap review** (generation + self-explanation): Open the report only to check against your recall; for each gap, generate a self-explanation of why you missed it and create a permanent note. (3) **Connection mapping** (elaboration): Open your vault and create bidirectional links from the new material to at least 3-5 existing permanent notes, writing 1-2 sentences explaining each connection. (4) **SRS card creation** (spacing + retrieval practice for future): Create Anki cards from the key concepts, ensuring question forms require generation rather than recognition. (5) **Set spaced review intervals**: Schedule a retrieval test at 1 day, 3 days, 1 week, 2 weeks, 1 month from reading.
> 
> **Source:** Section 7.2: Strategy Deployment in the Reading-to-PKB Workflow
> **Difficulty:** Application
> **Tags:** #reading-practice #pkb-workflow #seven-strategies

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Connection):** What is the relationship between the basic psychological needs (SDT) and mastery goal adoption (Achievement Goal Theory)?
> 
> **Answer:** Need satisfaction and mastery goals are bidirectionally reinforcing. Autonomy need satisfaction predicts mastery goal adoption: learners who experience their study as self-chosen and personally meaningful define success in terms of developing genuine understanding rather than social performance. Competence need satisfaction supports mastery goals by calibrating challenge to the growing edge, keeping failure informative rather than devastating. Conversely, mastery goals support need satisfaction: orienting toward competence development creates the contexts in which genuine improvement (competence need) and self-chosen challenge (autonomy need) are most likely to be experienced. Performance goals tend to frustrate both needs by making success contingent on social comparison rather than genuine development.
> 
> **Source:** Section 4.2: Achievement Goal Theory
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #self-determination-theory #achievement-goal-theory #motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Definition):** What is the Fluency Illusion, and what is its primary consequence for self-regulated reading?
> 
> **Answer:** The fluency illusion is the systematic tendency to mistake the cognitive ease of re-reading (increased familiarity = easier processing = faster reading) for a reliable signal of learning depth. The primary consequence for self-regulated reading is monitoring corruption: the monitoring phase of the SRL cycle receives false positive signals (high comprehension estimates) from re-reading, preventing the control phase from triggering the strategy adjustments (retrieval practice, self-explanation) that would actually produce durable encoding. Learners who rely heavily on re-reading are operating with calibrated monitoring for the wrong signal.
> 
> **Source:** Section 2.5: Prior Knowledge and the Fluency Illusion
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #fluency-illusion #metacognitive-monitoring #self-regulated-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Distinction):** Distinguish between the motivational costs of the testing effect, the spacing effect, and the interleaving effect. Why is each difficult for a different reason?
> 
> **Answer:** **Testing effect**: Motivationally costly because it provides immediate, undeniable evidence of forgetting and incompetence — it directly confronts the self-efficacy of learners with performance goals. **Spacing effect**: Motivationally costly because it requires deliberately *tolerating* forgetting between sessions — the subjective experience of apparently losing progress between reviews feels like evidence the strategy is failing, when it is actually the mechanism of its success. **Interleaving effect**: Motivationally costly because interleaved practice consistently produces *lower* subjective confidence and *lower* apparent performance than blocked practice during the session itself, even though it produces better long-term transfer. The interleaving problem is the starkest inversion of the performance-mastery feel: you feel less competent during the strategy that actually makes you most competent.
> 
> **Source:** Sections 3.2, 3.3, 3.4
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #desirable-difficulties #testing-effect #spacing-effect #interleaving

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the testing effect, and why does it produce better retention than re-studying?
> **A:** The testing effect is the finding that retrieving information from long-term memory produces stronger, more durable traces than equivalent re-studying. It works because: (1) retrieval builds the retrieval pathway specifically; (2) elaborative retrieval enriches encoding; (3) failed retrieval creates error-correction opportunities; (4) it is a desirable difficulty that engages consolidation processes.
> **Source:** Section 4, [[Testing-Effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion, and why is it a metacognitive problem?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is using processing ease (how smoothly text is read) as a cue for comprehension depth (how deeply it has been understood). It is a metacognitive monitoring error: the wrong signal (fluency) is feeding the judgment-of-learning system, producing systematic overconfidence in understanding and premature termination of study.
> **Source:** Sections 1 and 7, [[fluency-illusion]]
> **Difficulty:** Basic

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is a desirable difficulty, and what distinguishes it from undesirable difficulty?
> **A:** A desirable difficulty impedes performance during acquisition but produces better long-term retention and transfer by engaging constructive and reconstructive memory operations (retrieval, elaboration, discrimination). Undesirable difficulty impedes performance *without* engaging these operations — e.g., cramped fonts, poor organization, cognitive overload beyond competence zone.
> **Source:** Section 2, [[desirable-difficulties]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** How do spacing and retrieval practice interact, and why is the interaction important?
> **A:** Retrieval practice is substantially more powerful when combined with spacing: testing at a delay requires actual retrieval from long-term memory (implicit retrieval practice embedded in spaced study), while testing immediately draws on working memory. The combination — retrieval at optimally spaced intervals — is the theoretical foundation of spaced repetition systems.
> **Source:** Sections 4 and 5, [[spaced-repetition]], [[retrieval-practice]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the interleaving effect, and why does it feel less productive than blocked study?
> **A:** The interleaving effect is that mixing problem types or topics within a study session produces better long-term retention and transfer than studying each type exhaustively before moving to the next. It feels less productive because it prevents the shallow repetition fluency that blocked study generates — requiring re-encoding from scratch on each encounter, which is the source of both the felt difficulty and the learning benefit.
> **Source:** Section 5, [[interleaving-effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** According to Self-Determination Theory, what are the three basic psychological needs and their relevance to academic reading engagement?
> **A:** Autonomy (experiencing reading as self-chosen, identity-aligned), Competence (experiencing genuine mastery growth through challenge calibrated to current ability), and Relatedness (experiencing connection to intellectual community and purpose). All three must be satisfied for reading engagement to be sustainable and autonomously motivated rather than compliance-driven and fragile.
> **Source:** Section 8, [[basic-psychological-needs]], [[self-determination-theory]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the generation effect, and how does it differ from retrieval practice?
> **A:** The generation effect is the finding that self-generated information is encoded more durably than received information. Unlike retrieval practice (which retrieves already-encoded information), generation occurs *during initial encoding* — before durable storage. Both engage active construction, but at different points in the learning cycle: generation at encoding, retrieval practice during maintenance.
> **Source:** Section 3, [[generation-effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the self-explanation effect, and what is its primary mechanism?
> **A:** Students who generate more self-explanations during problem-solving and text study (articulating why claims are true, how processes work) show substantially greater learning gains than those who explain less. The primary mechanism is gap-detection: self-explanation forces the learner to confront logical gaps in their understanding that fluent reading conceals, triggering targeted repair of exactly the incomplete aspects of their mental model.
> **Source:** Section 6, [[self-explanation-effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three phases of Zimmerman's cyclical self-regulated learning model, and what is their relevance to strategic reading?
> **A:** Forethought (goal-setting, prior knowledge activation, strategy planning before reading), Performance (active strategy deployment with comprehension monitoring during reading), and Self-Reflection (honest post-reading evaluation of what was learned and strategy effectiveness). Most natural readers skip forethought and self-reflection entirely, which eliminates the cyclical improvement mechanism that makes SRL more effective over time.
> **Source:** Section 10, [[cyclical-model-of-self-regulated-learning]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** In the Nelson-Narens model of metacognition, what are monitoring and control, and how do they interact?
> **A:** Monitoring: information flowing from the object (cognitive) level to the meta level, including judgments of learning, feelings of knowing, and comprehension signals. Control: commands flowing from the meta level to the object level, adjusting strategies, allocating study time, deciding to review. Effective self-regulation requires both: monitoring that generates accurate signals, and control operations that respond to those signals adaptively.
> **Source:** Section 7, [[nelson-narens-model]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced





































































































## Key Figures & Intellectual Lineage

> [!person] **John Sweller (1946–)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Emeritus Professor, University of New South Wales, Australia. Developed Cognitive Load Theory beginning in 1988, originating the framework from research on problem-solving and the means-ends analysis strategy. Sweller's evolutionary psychology framing (biologically primary vs. secondary knowledge) provided CLT with its theoretical unity beyond empirical findings. His 2010 reconceptualization of germane load represented significant self-correction. CLT's application to instructional design is his primary legacy.

> [!person] **Fred Paas (1962–)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Professor, Erasmus University Rotterdam. Developed the Paas Cognitive Load Scale (1992), the most widely used measure of subjective mental effort in CLT research. Extensive work on self-assessment in learning, worked examples, and testing effects. Key collaborator with Sweller and van Merriënboer on the foundational CLT literature.

> [!person] **Jeroen van Merriënboer (1959–)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Professor, Maastricht University. Co-developer with Sweller and Paas of the CLT theoretical framework. Primary originator of the 4C/ID model (Four Component Instructional Design), the most systematic translation of CLT into practical instructional design methodology. Extensive work on complex learning, task authenticity, and whole-task practice.

> [!person] **Alan Baddeley (1934–)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Emeritus Professor, University of York, UK. Developed the multi-component model of working memory (1974, with Graham Hitch), which underwent major elaborations in 2000 (adding the episodic buffer). Baddeley's model is the working memory architecture that CLT implicitly adopts, providing the empirical basis for the phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and central executive distinctions that inform multimodal instructional design.

> [!person] **Niklas Luhmann (1927–1998)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> German sociologist and intellectual of extraordinary scope. Developed the Zettelkasten note-making method over a 40-year academic career producing over 70 books and 400 articles. Though not a learning scientist, Luhmann anticipated several CLT insights through his methodological practice: atomicity, explicit link annotation, distributed network rather than hierarchical organization. Luhmann's Zettelkasten is the empirical existence proof that an externalized associative network can amplify intellectual productivity in ways consistent with CLT's schema-formation requirements.

> [!person] **John Sweller (b. 1946) — University of New South Wales** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Creator of Cognitive Load Theory. Sweller's foundational contribution was demonstrating experimentally that conventional problem-solving practice imposes higher cognitive load and produces less learning than studying worked examples (1985, 1988). He subsequently developed the theoretical framework linking cognitive architecture to instructional design, introduced element interactivity as the theory's central predictive construct, and most recently reconceptualized the three-factor model and introduced the evolutionary framing of biologically primary/secondary knowledge. His willingness to revise his own theory (the 2010 germane load reconceptualization) exemplifies intellectual honesty in theoretical development.
> 
> **Key works:** Sweller (1988), Sweller et al. (1998), Sweller (2010), Sweller (2011)

> [!person] **Fred Paas (b. 1963) — Erasmus University Rotterdam** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Co-developer of the three-factor model and pioneer of cognitive load measurement methodology. [[fred-paas|Paas]] developed the widely used 9-point mental effort rating scale (1992) and co-authored the influential reformulation distinguishing intrinsic, extraneous, and germane load. His work on measurement has been foundational for empirical CLT research, even as the limitations of subjective measurement continue to challenge the field.
> 
> **Key works:** Paas (1992), Sweller, van Merriënboer & Paas (1998), Paas et al. (2003)

> [!person] **Jeroen van Merriënboer (b. 1959) — Maastricht University** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Co-developer of the three-factor model and creator of the [[four-component-instructional-design-4cid|4C/ID (Four-Component Instructional Design)]] model. [[jeroen-van-merriënboer|Van Merriënboer]] translated CLT principles into a comprehensive instructional design methodology for complex learning environments, bridging the gap between theory and practice. He has also maintained the theoretical value of the germane load construct against Sweller's reconceptualization.
> 
> **Key works:** van Merriënboer (1997), Sweller, van Merriënboer & Paas (1998), van Merriënboer & Kirschner (2018)

> [!person] **Sergei Kalyuga — University of New South Wales** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Principal researcher on the [[expertise-reversal-effect|expertise reversal effect]] and adaptive instruction. [[Sergei-Kalyuga|Kalyuga's]] work demonstrated that virtually all CLT effects reverse with increasing expertise, establishing one of the theory's most important boundary conditions and motivating the research program on adaptive CLT-based instruction.
> 
> **Key works:** Kalyuga et al. (2003), Kalyuga (2007), Kalyuga (2011)

> [!person] **Richard Mayer (b. 1947) — UC Santa Barbara** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Developer of the [[cognitive-theory-of-multimedia-learning]], which independently derives many principles parallel to CLT effects from similar cognitive architecture assumptions. [[Richard-Mayer|Mayer's]] work on multimedia learning principles has provided the most extensive applied evidence base for CLT-adjacent design principles, and his research program has been instrumental in extending cognitive load considerations into technology-enhanced learning environments.
> 
> **Key works:** Mayer (2001, 2009), Mayer & Moreno (2003)

> [!person] **Paul Chandler — University of New South Wales (deceased)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Sweller's key early collaborator who co-developed the theoretical treatment of split attention and redundancy effects. Chandler's work was instrumental in establishing CLT's empirical effects catalog during the 1990s.
> 
> **Key works:** Chandler & Sweller (1991), Sweller & Chandler (1994)

> [!person] **Jean Piaget (1896–1980)** *(from [[constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Position:** Swiss psychologist and genetic epistemologist; foundational theorist of cognitive constructivism
> **Core contribution:** The assimilation-accommodation-equilibration model of cognitive development; the identification of four developmental stages; the concept of schemas as units of knowledge; the clinical method for investigating children's reasoning.
> **Intellectual lineage:** Influenced by Kant (active cognition), Bergson (vitalism), James Mark Baldwin (developmental epistemology). Influenced von Glasersfeld (radical constructivism), Bruner (process of education), Ausubel (meaningful learning theory), Inhelder (formal operations research).
> **Key works:** *The Language and Thought of the Child* (1923); *The Construction of Reality in the Child* (1937); *The Origins of Intelligence in Children* (1952); *Genetic Epistemology* (1970)
> *See also:* [[jean-piaget]], [[genetic-epistemology]], [[assimilation-and-accommodation]]

> [!person] **Lev Vygotsky (1896–1934)** *(from [[constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Position:** Soviet psychologist; founder of social constructivism and cultural-historical psychology
> **Core contribution:** The zone of proximal development; the social origins of higher mental functions; language as the primary tool of thought; the internalization of social processes as individual cognitive capacities.
> **Intellectual lineage:** Influenced by Marx (dialectical materialism), Engels, Spinoza, Piaget (against whom he argued). Influenced Wood, Bruner & Ross (scaffolding), Lave & Wenger (situated learning), Wertsch (mediated action), Engeström (activity theory).
> **Key works:** *Thought and Language* (1934/1962); *Mind in Society* (1978); *The Collected Works of L. S. Vygotsky* (6 vols.)
> *See also:* [[lev-vygotsky]], [[scaffolding]], [[situated-learning]]

> [!person] **Jerome Bruner (1915–2016)** *(from [[constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Position:** American cognitive psychologist; Harvard, New York University; key figure in cognitive revolution and constructivist education
> **Core contribution:** Spiral curriculum; discovery learning; enactive-iconic-symbolic representation modes; narrative as a mode of thought; cultural psychology.
> **Key works:** *The Process of Education* (1960); *Toward a Theory of Instruction* (1966); *Actual Minds, Possible Worlds* (1986); *The Culture of Education* (1996)
> *See also:* [[jerome-bruner]], [[discovery-learning]], [[generative-learning-theory]]

> [!person] **Ernst von Glasersfeld (1917–2010)** *(from [[constructivist-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Position:** Austrian-American cyberneticist and epistemologist; primary theorist of radical constructivism
> **Core contribution:** Radical constructivism — the epistemological position that knowledge is always a viability-driven construction rather than a representation of mind-independent reality; the distinction between viable and veridical knowledge.
> **Intellectual lineage:** Influenced by Piaget, Kant, Heinz von Foerster (cybernetics), Giambattista Vico.
> **Key works:** *Radical Constructivism: A Way of Knowing and Learning* (1995); *The Construction of Knowledge* (1987)
> *See also:* [[radical-constructivism]], [[genetic-epistemology]], [[ernst-von-glasersfeld]]

> [!person] **Endel Tulving (b. 1927)** *(from [[encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> Estonian-Canadian cognitive psychologist; Rotman Professor Emeritus at the University of Toronto. Developed the episodic/semantic memory distinction (1972), formulated the Encoding Specificity Principle with Thomson (1973), introduced the SPI model and synergistic ecphory in *Elements of Episodic Memory* (1983), and conducted extensive research on consciousness and memory. Widely considered the most influential memory theorist of the 20th century's second half. Key work: "Encoding specificity and retrieval processes in episodic memory" (1973); *Elements of Episodic Memory* (1983); "Memory and consciousness" (1985).
> 
> **Relationship to other figures in this report:** Collaborated with Donald Thomson; in productive tension with Craik and Lockhart's LOP framework (responding to Craik and Lockhart 1972); indirectly in dialogue with Bransford and colleagues (TAP); revived and formalized Semon's (1904) ecphory concept.

> [!person] **Donald M. Thomson** *(from [[encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> Australian cognitive psychologist; co-author with Tulving of the foundational 1973 *Psychological Review* paper. Thomson's contribution to the ESP was both theoretical and empirical: his experimental designs were instrumental in producing the recognition-failure-of-recallable-words paradigm. His work also extended the ESP into applied contexts including forensic memory and eyewitness testimony.
> 
> **Relationship to other figures:** Joint primary author with Tulving; the ESP is sometimes called the Tulving-Thomson principle in recognition of this collaboration.

> [!person] **Fergus I. M. Craik (b. 1935)** *(from [[encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> British-Canadian cognitive psychologist; co-developer with Robert Lockhart of the Levels of Processing framework (1972), which stands in productive tension with the ESP. Craik subsequently collaborated with Tulving (1975) on research attempting to synthesize LOP and the ESP. His work on depth of processing, elaboration, and distinctive encoding provides a complementary rather than competing account of encoding effects.
> 
> **Relationship to other figures:** Co-created LOP with Lockhart; empirically engaged with Tulving's framework rather than dismissing it; the 1975 Craik & Tulving paper is an important attempt at synthesis.

> [!person] **John D. Bransford (b. 1944)** *(from [[encoding-specificity-principle-tulving-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> American cognitive psychologist; co-author (with Morris and Franks) of the 1977 Transfer-Appropriate Processing paper. Bransford's work consistently emphasized constructive processes in learning and memory, connecting to the Bransford & Johnson (1972) contextual schema research that showed how a sentence's meaning — and hence memorability — depended on activating the right contextual frame at encoding.
> 
> **Relationship to other figures:** The Bransford & Johnson (1972) study is a closely related precursor to formal TAP. TAP stands in functional dialogue with the ESP, sharing its core encoding-retrieval compatibility claim while differing in theoretical emphasis.

> [!person] **Merlin Wittrock (1931–2007)** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> Educational psychologist at UCLA, the originator of Generative Learning Theory. Wittrock's 1974 paper "A Generative Model of Mathematics Education" introduced the core thesis, which he subsequently elaborated and extended through the 1990s. His 1989 chapter "Generative Processes of Comprehension" is the canonical theoretical statement. Wittrock drew on Piaget's constructivism, cognitive science's information-processing revolution, and Rothkopf's mathemagenic concept to develop a uniquely learner-centered theory of cognitive engagement with educational content.

> [!person] **Richard Mayer (1947–)** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> Educational psychologist at UC Santa Barbara and the primary architect of the Cognitive Theory of Multimedia Learning, which extends Wittrock's GLT to multimedia environments using Baddeley's dual-channel memory model. Mayer has authored over 500 publications and multiple landmark books including *Multimedia Learning* (2001, 2009), producing the most extensive empirical program in educational psychology's history. His CTML is the leading framework for evidence-based instructional design in multimedia contexts.

> [!person] **Michelene Chi (1950–)** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> Cognitive scientist at Arizona State University whose research on self-explanation, knowledge revision, and the ICAP framework has profoundly extended GLT theory and application. Chi's 1989 paper documenting the self-explanation effect opened a major research program; her 2014 ICAP framework provided a comprehensive taxonomy of learning engagement levels.

> [!person] **Manu Kapur** *(from [[generative-learning-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> Educational psychologist whose research on productive failure challenged the conventional assumption that successful performance should precede independent practice. Kapur's experimental demonstrations that pre-instruction problem exploration improves subsequent learning provided an important counter-intuitive extension of generative learning principles to instructional sequencing.

> [!person] **Hermann Ebbinghaus (1850–1909)** *(from [[long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> German psychologist; pioneer of the experimental study of memory. Using himself as subject and nonsense syllables as material, Ebbinghaus established the [[Forgetting-Curve|forgetting curve]], the [[spacing-effect|spacing effect]], and the [[Learning-Curve|learning curve]] — three of the most fundamental regularities in memory research. His 1885 monograph *Über das Gedächtnis* inaugurated the quantitative, laboratory-based tradition of memory research.
> **Key work:** *Über das Gedächtnis* (1885).

> [!person] **Frederic Bartlett (1886–1969)** *(from [[long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> British psychologist; Cambridge professor who pioneered the study of [[reconstructive-memory|reconstructive memory]]. Bartlett demonstrated that remembering is an active, constructive process shaped by the rememberer's schemas, expectations, and cultural frameworks. His 1932 *Remembering* directly opposed the Ebbinghaus tradition's emphasis on verbatim retention, introducing the concept of the schema and establishing the constructive paradigm.
> **Key work:** *Remembering: A Study in Experimental and Social Psychology* (1932).
> **Relationship:** Intellectual counterpoint to Ebbinghaus; forerunner of Loftus's misinformation research.

> [!person] **Endel Tulving (1927–2023)** *(from [[long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> Estonian-Canadian cognitive psychologist; developed the distinction between [[episodic-memory|episodic]] and [[semantic-memory|semantic memory]] (1972), the [[encoding-specificity-principle|encoding specificity principle]] (1973), and the concept of autonoetic consciousness. Tulving's taxonomic contributions provided the conceptual framework for the modern multiple memory systems approach.
> **Key works:** *Elements of Episodic Memory* (1983); "Episodic and semantic memory" (1972).
